Problem: Stakeholders demand elimination of the credit service’s 30 percentage-point true positive rate (TPR) disparity. What is the “Price of Fairness” in lending outcomes?

Physics: TPRs can be equalized by adjusting the classification threshold \((\ClassificationThreshold)\) for the disadvantaged group.

  • Original state: Group A (\(\text{TPR} =\) 90 percent), Group B (\(\text{TPR} =\) 60 percent, \(\text{FPR} =\) 20 percent).
  • Intervention: Lower \(\DisadvantagedGroupClassificationThreshold\) until \(\text{TPR}_{\SubgroupIndex=\text{B}} =\) 90 percent. (US credit law restricts group-specific cutoffs, as the mitigation discussion noted, so the calculation quantifies the trade-off rather than a deployable intervention.)
  • The cost: Lowering the threshold increases false positives (approving applicants who later default).

Math:

  1. Under the scenario’s assumed threshold response, closing the 30 percentage-point TPR gap produces a 20 percentage-point increase in false positives for the disadvantaged group.
  2. If 50 percent of Group B applicants would repay, a repaid loan earns a margin of $1,500, and a default loses $3,000, both sides of the intervention must be counted:
    • Group B baseline utility evaluates the original expected payoff: \(0.60 \times 0.50 \times \$1{,}500 - 0.20 \times 0.50 \times \$3{,}000 = \$150\) per applicant.
    • Evaluating the intervention requires counting both marginal terms: \(\FiniteChange\text{Utility} = \FiniteChange\text{TPR} \times \text{Base Rate} \times \text{Loan Margin} - \FiniteChange\text{FPR} \times (1 - \text{Base Rate}) \times \text{Default Loss}\).
    • The added true-positive value is $225 per Group B applicant, while the added false-positive cost is $300, producing a net loss of $75.
    • Dividing this $75 net loss by the $150 baseline yields a 50 percent within-group utility loss. With Group B at 17 percent of applicants, the population-average loss is $12.50 per applicant; calculating an aggregate percentage also requires the other group’s baseline utility.

Systems insight: The “Price of Fairness”, the fairness literature’s name for the utility a system gives up to satisfy a fairness constraint, in this scenario is a 50 percent within-group utility loss, not a derivable aggregate percentage. The loss is not automatic: when a TPR gap reflects a miscalibrated threshold, closing it can raise net utility. It appears when the baseline threshold is near the utility optimum and the marginal approvals skew toward default. Engineering teams owe stakeholders both the Pareto frontier and the operational assumptions that produced it.

The calculation gives stakeholders a way to choose and defend a point on the fairness-accuracy frontier, where Group B’s 60 percent true positive rate is weighed against Group A’s 90 percent rate, but cohort-level release metrics live inside the engineering team. Once the model ships, neither the applicant it rejects nor a downstream team that inherits the checkpoint can act on a cohort average; both need the system’s individual decisions and its operating boundaries made legible.

15.3.3 Explain and document: Decisions and models made legible

When the credit service rejects a borrower with a bare probability score of 0.41, the applicant cannot tell whether the denial came from a high debt-to-income ratio or a corrupted credit-bureau field. Six months later, another team reuses that same classifier to screen rental tenants without knowing which populations it was tested on, and the organization inherits a second failure at the model level. Explanation and documentation answer that single demand for legibility at two timescales. An explanation makes one decision understandable, at the moment it is made, to the person it affects. Documentation makes the model understandable across its lifetime to the engineers, auditors, and downstream teams who inherit it: what it was trained on, where it was validated, and what it must not be used for. Both exist for the same systems reason, since a guarantee that no one outside the team can inspect cannot be contested, audited, or safely reused.

Explainability requirements

At the decision timescale, an applicant denied with that 0.41 score may be entitled under applicable law to specific adverse-action factors that state which inputs drove the rejection and what could change it. Explainability10 is the capability that articulates how input features contributed to a specific prediction, enabling human oversight of automated decisions, supporting incident debugging, and satisfying applicable requirements for decision transparency.

10 Explainability and interpretability: Interpretability usually describes how readily a person can understand a model or its behavior, often through a constrained structure such as a short rule list or sparse linear model. Explainability is broader and includes post-hoc methods such as LIME and SHAP. Neither property is automatic: a linear model with opaque features can be difficult to interpret, and a feature attribution is not a causal account of a decision. The systems implication is that intrinsic constraints affect model selection, while post-hoc methods add a computation and validation path whose cost depends on the method, model, and serving workflow. GDPR access and automated-decision provisions use the phrase “meaningful information about the logic involved” for covered processing (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2016), leaving the appropriate technical approach dependent on the decision and governing law.

The level of explainability required varies by application context and regulatory environment (table 8), and the requirement constrains architecture rather than logging. Where credit underwriting may require principal reasons disclosed to the applicant and medical diagnosis requires clinical review support, fraud detection must balance notice duties against adversarial gaming. Serving an outside reader raises the bar beyond the SHAP values that localized mispredictions during incident debugging, because the explanation requirement determines before training which model families are eligible and what additional computation the serving path must carry.

↩︎ SHAP values, which estimate how much each input feature contributed to one prediction, are established as a debugging tool in Feature attribution for debugging.

Whatever the requirement, the candidate models lie along a gradient continuum from intrinsic interpretability to post-hoc explanation (figure 4). On the left, small trees and sparse linear or logistic models with meaningful features can expose decision logic to human inspection. Large trees or opaque engineered features can defeat that advantage. On the right, high-capacity representations, such as random forests, deep neural networks, and convolutional neural networks (CNNs) or transformers, often need supplementary explanation methods. LIME and SHAP require fidelity checks; attention maps alone do not establish faithful explanations. The spectrum organizes candidate approaches, not a categorical guarantee for any model family.

Selecting an explainability strategy along this spectrum requires balancing explanation fidelity, serving latency, and architectural flexibility across three distinct design paradigms. Post-hoc explanation methods, including SHAP and LIME, generate feature importance scores for individual predictions without modifying model internals.11 Model-agnostic explainers evaluate perturbed inputs or sampled feature subsets, introducing additional computation and approximation. Model-specific SHAP algorithms can exploit internal structure instead; the explanation method and where it runs determine latency and fidelity.

11 LIME (local interpretable model-agnostic explanations) and SHAP (SHapley additive explanations): LIME (Ribeiro et al. 2016) fits a local interpretable surrogate around each prediction; SHAP (Lundberg and Lee 2017) adapts Shapley values from cooperative game theory to compute feature contributions under a unified additive framework. Exact Shapley computation requires evaluating a feature’s marginal contribution across all possible feature subsets, scaling exponentially with the feature count \(\AttributionFeatureCount\) (\(\mathcal{O}(2^{\AttributionFeatureCount})\)); practical production deployments therefore rely on polynomial-time model-specific algorithms (such as TreeSHAP for decision trees) or sampling approximations (such as KernelSHAP) to avoid unacceptable serving latency. The systems trade-off is that explanation fidelity, latency, and implementation complexity must be budgeted explicitly rather than treated as free.

Ribeiro, Marco Tulio, Sameer Singh, and Carlos Guestrin. 2016. “Why Should I Trust You?: Explaining the Predictions of Any Classifier.” Proceedings of the 22nd ACM SIGKDD International Conference on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining, 1135–44. https://doi.org/10.1145/2939672.2939778.
Lundberg, Scott M., and Su-In Lee. 2017. “A Unified Approach to Interpreting Model Predictions.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30: 4765–74.

The explanation path has a measurable serving cost. For the credit service’s 20 input features, exact Shapley attribution evaluates the model on every feature subset, 1,048,576 evaluations to explain a decision that took one evaluation to make. Sampling explainers bound that work by evaluating a fixed number of subsets, but even 1,000 samples multiply the compute of every explained decision by the sample count. The explanation path rather than the model can therefore set the service’s accelerator budget and its tail latency.

For the rejected credit application, a LIME explanation is dependable only if its local surrogate agrees with the model near the applicant and stays stable across resampling. The check fits the surrogate on perturbed applications drawn from a neighborhood of plausible applications (valid feature ranges, with dependencies such as income and debt-to-income ratio preserved). It then measures the surrogate’s prediction error and approval agreement against the model on an independent set from the same neighborhood. The check also repeats the fit across sampling seeds and neighborhood widths to see whether the leading features and their signs persist. Acceptable error and stability must be specified before the check, and even a faithful surrogate describes model behavior without supplying a causal explanation or a legally sufficient reason.

Avoiding that per-request attribution cost moves the design to the left side of the interpretability spectrum, where inherently interpretable models enforce transparency through structural constraints such as shallow decision trees, sparse linear models, or generalized additive models with inspectable feature effects, trading away expressive capacity on high-dimensional inputs when the feature set stays compact and meaningful. Where inherently interpretable models constrain the architecture from the start, a third paradigm, concept-based explanations, probes what a trained network has already learned. It projects internal activations onto directions derived from labeled examples of a human concept, such as a “striped texture” direction in a vision model. The projection shows whether a representation encodes the concept; whether the prediction uses it requires a further test of how the output changes along that direction, and every concept needs its own annotated example set.

Example 15.1: The hospital shortcut
Scenario: Pneumonia detection vision models trained at Mount Sinai achieved high internal accuracy but failed on external hospital datasets (Zech et al. 2018).

Diagnosis: The neural network learned shortcut features (hospital-specific scanner artifacts and text tags) rather than true biological lung pathology.

Systems lesson: A model can learn whichever feature predicts the label most easily, including one unrelated to the disease. External validation is essential for detecting shortcut learning. Saliency maps, per-pixel heat maps of how strongly each input region influences the output, can provide supporting evidence but do not establish that the model learned the intended mechanism.

Zech, John R., Marcus A. Badgeley, Manway Liu, Anthony B. Costa, Joseph J. Titano, and Eric Karl Oermann. 2018. “Variable Generalization Performance of a Deep Learning Model to Detect Pneumonia in Chest Radiographs: A Cross-Sectional Study.” PLOS Medicine 15 (11): e1002683. https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pmed.1002683.

The hospital shortcut shows why interpretability is a systems requirement rather than presentation polish. Teams need sufficient transparency to detect and isolate spurious correlations before deployment. The choice among these paradigms depends on the application’s accountability requirements. Credit law requires specific reasons for covered decisions but does not mandate a particular model architecture, and other applications face different transparency, safety, or contestability duties. Section 15.5.3 maps those duties to the capabilities they demand. The spectrum does not imply “simple is always better,” because a highly interpretable model that makes inaccurate predictions may also cause harm. An explanation, however, covers one decision at a time. The assumptions behind every decision the model makes need a durable record of their own, one that travels with the artifact.

Model documentation standards

When a team inherits a production model without that durable record, the artifact may post 94 percent accuracy on a designated test set while leaving the origin of the test set, the composition of the training data, and the evaluated demographic or environmental slices unrecorded. Updating or redeploying the model then risks unmeasured regressions under distribution shift. Model cards provide standardized documentation for trained models12 (Mitchell et al. 2019). Analogous to component datasheets in computer engineering, model cards document operational boundaries, evaluation conditions, and intended operating regimes alongside the model artifact.

12 Model cards: The primary failure mode model cards address is scope creep, the gradual expansion from “it worked for case A” to “try it for case B” without revalidating intended use. In practice, cards are often written after deployment decisions are made, documenting observed behavior rather than constraining it. The companion “Datasheets for Datasets” (Gebru et al. 2021) applies the same principle to training data. Without both, the card becomes a historical record rather than an engineering guardrail.

Gebru, Timnit, Jamie Morgenstern, Briana Vecchione, Jennifer Wortman Vaughan, Hanna Wallach, Hal Daumé III, and Kate Crawford. 2021. “Datasheets for Datasets.” Communications of the ACM 64 (12): 86–92. https://doi.org/10.1145/3458723.
Mitchell, Margaret, Simone Wu, Andrew Zaldivar, Parker Barnes, Lucy Vasserman, Ben Hutchinson, Elena Spitzer, Inioluwa Deborah Raji, and Timnit Gebru. 2019. “Model Cards for Model Reporting.” Proceedings of the Conference on Fairness, Accountability, and Transparency, 220–29. https://doi.org/10.1145/3287560.3287596.

A complete model card covers seven core sections that together define the model’s safe operating envelope. It begins with technical details (architecture, training procedures, and hyperparameters) that enable reproducibility and auditing. It specifies intended use alongside explicit out-of-scope applications, preventing scope creep where models trained for consumer photo indexing get repurposed for high-stakes surveillance. The card then documents which factors (demographic groups, environmental conditions, or sensor variations) affect performance, guiding both evaluation slicing and production monitoring.

The remaining sections quantify and bound empirical behavior. Performance metrics must report disaggregated results across the operational factors identified in section 15.3.2, because aggregate accuracy conceals systematic subpopulation disparities. Documenting the training and evaluation datasets isolates potential sampling biases and defines the distribution over which the reported metrics hold. Finally, ethical considerations, caveats, and recommendations make deployment trade-offs explicit by recording known failure modes, unmodeled edge cases, and operational mitigations.

Applying this schema to MobileNetV2 illustrates how these categories translate into concrete deployment constraints. Table 9 fills five of the seven sections for an edge classification service, and its accuracy entries reuse the illustrative FP32 and INT8 values of the compression validation in Compression validation: The efficiency-quality frontier.

Datasheets for datasets provide the complementary record for the data tier (Gebru et al. 2021), capturing provenance, collection methodology, curation filters, and demographic composition. In D·A·M terms, a model card specifies the algorithmic and machine boundaries (including quantization precision and inference latency budgets), and a datasheet specifies the data that produced them. Together they state what the system is designed to tolerate and preserve the evidence that measurement produced, but both describe the system at the moment of release. Operation is the stage that keeps that description true once the model meets live traffic.

↩︎ Datasheets, as part of the governance pillar of a data pipeline, are established in Four foundational pillars.

15.3.4 Operate: Fairness as loop stability

A credit model that passes its 5-percentage-point fairness gate and ships with a complete model card at launch can still breach that gate six quarters later without a single code change, because every quarterly retraining learns only from the applicants the previous model approved. Release gates and documentation certify the system at one instant, before live traffic and closed-loop retraining shift its behavior over time. Operating the service responsibly requires the incident-response sequence that operations already runs (detection, assessment, mitigation, communication, and remediation) to treat a fairness violation or a slice-level regression as an incident in its own right. Operations must also verify each control before launch, including alert thresholds tested against historical replay, a rollback path exercised under load, and an on-call rotation with documented escalation, so that monitoring and response exist before the model takes production traffic.

The feedback begins at the decision that determines which outcomes can be observed. Figure 5 distinguishes the routine repayment stream from a declared intervention that obtains additional observations.

An absent repayment label cannot be treated as a default or a successful repayment. An audit of existing records cannot reveal how a loan that was never issued would have performed. Obtaining evidence outside the routine approval stream requires an explicitly justified observation strategy, with its selection policy and follow-up recorded; estimating population disparities still depends on what that evidence supports.

↩︎ The ML incident-response sequence of severity classification, detection, impact assessment, mitigation, and root-cause analysis is established in Incident response for ML systems.

Sculley, D., Gary Holt, Daniel Golovin, Eugene Davydov, Todd Phillips, Dietmar Ebner, Vinay Chaudhary, Michael Young, Jean-François Crespo, and Dan Dennison. 2015. “Hidden Technical Debt in Machine Learning Systems.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 28: 2503–11.

Where service-health monitoring catches crashes and timeouts, the technical debt that ML systems accumulate surfaces as silent degradation (Sculley et al. 2015), so the production telemetry surface must add two responsibility signals alongside input drift and delayed outcomes. Subgroup parity becomes a first-class monitor, tracking false-negative and false-positive rates per cohort over time with response plans that account for label delay, while user complaints, contested decisions, and appeals feed the same surface because aggregation over millions of queries smooths away the rare harms that affected people report first. Parity monitoring matters most when the system’s decisions shape its own future training data. In that regime the bias feedback model (principle 13) describes a disparity that evolves as \(\GroupDisparity(\FeedbackIntervalCount) \approx \GroupDisparity(0) \cdot \BiasFeedbackFactor^\FeedbackIntervalCount\) across feedback intervals rather than staying fixed, and the credit service shows what that recurrence implies for a release gate.

↩︎ Production monitoring architectures for performance stability and distribution drift are established in Production Monitoring.

Napkin Math 15.3: Fairness as loop stability
Problem: After mitigation, the credit service from section 15.3.2.1 ships with a residual TPR gap of 3 percentage points, inside the scenario’s 5 percentage-point release gate. Denied applicants generate no repayment labels in the routine training stream, so each quarterly retraining learns from a population the previous model selected. For this illustration, an independent audit sample provides observed repayment outcomes across both groups, including applicants not approved by the production policy. Without such outcome evidence or a justified missing-outcome correction, the all-qualified-applicant TPR gap would not be directly measurable. The next quarterly audit measures a gap of 3.3 percentage points. If the locally fitted growth factor persisted, when would the gap breach the release threshold?

Math:

  1. Fit the feedback factor: \(\BiasFeedbackFactor= \GroupDisparity(1)/\GroupDisparity(0) =\) 1.10 per quarter.
  2. Time to breach under the fitted model: The projected gap exceeds the gate once \(\GroupDisparity(0)\,\BiasFeedbackFactor^\FeedbackIntervalCount\) passes it, that is, for \(\FeedbackIntervalCount> \ln(\text{gate}/\GroupDisparity(0)) / \ln \BiasFeedbackFactor\). The model projects a breach after 6 quarterly retraining cycles, when the gap reaches 5.3 percentage points.
  3. Without mitigation: The same factor would grow the original 30 percentage-point gap to 43.9 percentage points within 4 cycles, one year of retraining.
  4. Changing the loop: If the team approves a small randomized sample of marginal applicants and observes their repayment, the loop again sees outcomes it previously censored. Suppose the refitted factor falls to 0.90; the same launch gap then decays to 1.6 percentage points over the same number of cycles.

Systems insight: A release gate passed at launch is a statement about \(\FeedbackIntervalCount= 0\). Under feedback, fairness is a stability property of the retraining loop. The projected breach time motivates a shorter audit interval, but two observations do not validate future dynamics. An intervention can change \(\BiasFeedbackFactor\) (through what data the loop collects) as well as \(\GroupDisparity(0)\). The fitted factor is a local model; it must be refitted at every audit because intervention, saturation, or a shifting applicant pool changes the dynamics.

The DLRM ranking service runs the same loop with far more severe censoring. With 10 million daily users each shown 20 ranked items from a catalog of 1 million items, the service logs 200 million impressions a day. Yet each user’s feedback covers only 0.002 percent of the catalog, and the previous model chose every item in it. Items and creators the ranker stops showing generate no clicks and therefore no evidence that they deserved to be shown, which is the exposure form of the bias feedback model. The remedy mirrors the credit service’s randomized approvals. Reserving 1 percent of slots for randomized exploration yields 2 million impressions a day that the previous model did not select, a sample from which each creator group’s exposure share and engagement can be estimated against an unbiased baseline. The cost is measurable like any other serving change, because that share of slots is served at lower expected engagement, and it is what keeping the feedback factor below one costs.

Neither the credit service’s quarterly parity audits nor the DLRM service’s exploration slice protects users if the resulting signals sit on a passive dashboard. Both loops stay bounded only when scheduled audit cadences, clear on-call ownership, and explicit escalation thresholds tie an observed rise in \(\BiasFeedbackFactor\) or a drop in creator exposure directly to a retraining hold, an exploration adjustment, or a rollback.

Checkpoint 15.3: Ethical deployment

Deployment is where safeguards must become operational.

Safety net

Monitoring plan

Equitable performance across demographic cohorts addresses only one dimension of responsible engineering. The resource stage of the lifecycle addresses another, because every training run, inference request, and monitoring pipeline draws electrical power that converts to carbon emissions and operating expenditure. A pipeline can satisfy demographic parity while consuming orders of magnitude more compute than the task warrants, so responsible engineering must govern both whom the system serves and the physical resources consumed in serving them.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An engineering team is evaluating a facial verification model. To estimate the error rate of a minority demographic group representing 1% of the population with a margin of error of \(\pm 1\) percentage point at 95% confidence, they require 10,000 labeled evaluation samples from that group. Under uniform random sampling from the natural population distribution, how many total images must the team collect and label in expectation?

    1. About 10,000 total images, because evaluating subgroup accuracy requires only that the total test set contains 10,000 images.
    2. About 100,000 total images, because statistical confidence intervals scale with the square root of the overall dataset size.
    3. About 1,000,000 total images in expectation, because a 1% subgroup yields only 1 target image per 100 randomly sampled images, imposing a \(100\times\) multiplier.
    4. About 10,000,000 total images, because the binomial confidence interval width expands exponentially for minority subgroups.

  2. A team plans to write their model card six months after launch so that it accurately reflects observed production behavior. Explain why this timing constitutes a guard-rail failure, and describe one concrete scope-creep risk that a pre-deployment model card with automated deployment gates prevents.

  3. A loan approval classifier is evaluated on a fully labeled sample whose repayment outcomes are observed independently of its approval predictions. Group A (Majority): 4,500 True Positives, 500 False Negatives (TPR = 90%), 1,000 False Positives, 4,000 True Negatives (FPR = 20%). Group B (Minority): 600 True Positives, 400 False Negatives (TPR = 60%), 200 False Positives, 800 True Negatives (FPR = 20%). Which statement accurately diagnoses the fairness metrics for this system?

    1. Demographic parity is satisfied because both groups share an identical False Positive Rate of 20%.
    2. Equalized odds is satisfied because matching False Positive Rates compensate for differences in True Positive Rates.
    3. Equal opportunity is violated due to the 30 percentage-point TPR gap, and equalized odds is also violated because equalized odds strictly requires parity in both TPR and FPR.
    4. Calibration is the only metric affected, because True Positive Rate disparities impact accuracy but do not constitute algorithmic bias.

  4. For the credit service, closing the 30 percentage-point TPR gap for Group B via threshold adjustment adds \(\$225\) in margin on newly approved loans that are repaid but creates \(\$300\) in losses on newly approved loans that default, per Group B applicant, whose baseline utility is \(\$150\) per applicant. Using the chapter’s two-sided accounting, calculate the net utility change per applicant and explain what deliverable engineers owe stakeholders.

  5. After mitigation, the credit service ships with a 3 percentage-point TPR gap under a 5-point release gate, and the next quarterly audit measures 3.3 points. Using the bias feedback model, estimate the fitted feedback factor and the retraining cycle in which the projected gap first exceeds the gate. Why does the projection motivate changing what the loop observes rather than only shortening the audit interval?

  6. A team is designing a fairness and robustness testing suite for the credit service. Explain why subgroup representation and labeled sample sufficiency must be checked before interpreting subgroup error rates. How do invariance tests and boundary/stress tests complement those rates, and when can the team compare candidate configurations on a fairness-accuracy Pareto frontier?

See Answers →

15.4 Environmental and Cost Awareness

In 2019, researchers estimated that development-scale training and architecture search for a large natural language processing (NLP) model could emit as much carbon as five cars over their entire lifetimes (Strubell et al. 2019). Later analysis showed that the most quoted architecture-search estimate was highly sensitive to proxy-task, hardware, data-center efficiency, and grid carbon-intensity assumptions (Patterson et al. 2021). The correction narrows the estimate without removing the point. Training runs consume megawatt-hours of electricity, inference at scale multiplies per-request inefficiencies into measurable environmental impact, and resource-intensive models exclude organizations that lack large compute budgets. The techniques that improve efficiency therefore also serve as instruments of responsible engineering, tying computational efficiency to environmental sustainability and economic accessibility. On the DLRM ranking service, request volume makes serving rather than training the term that dominates both the bill and the emissions.

Strubell, Emma, Ananya Ganesh, and Andrew McCallum. 2019. “Energy and Policy Considerations for Deep Learning in NLP.” Proceedings of the 57th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics, 3645–50. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/p19-1355.

15.4.1 Efficiency as responsibility

Training a single large language model consumes thousands of GPU hours and energy measured in megawatt-hours. The bitter lesson predicts that this footprint will keep growing, because the methods that win are the ones that convert additional compute into capability. Much of this expense, however, is not intrinsic to the learning task but represents accidental complexity: training from scratch when fine-tuning would suffice, deploying larger architectures than tasks require, and running hyperparameter searches that explore redundant configurations. Computational cost depends on engineering choices as well as model physics. Green AI treats that efficiency as a primary metric rather than an afterthought.13

13 Green AI: Schwartz et al. (2020) contrasted “Red AI” (performance at any cost) with “Green AI” (efficiency as primary metric). The compute-growth anchor comes from AI and Compute’s 2012–2018 trend analysis (the fit that gives a 3.4-month doubling time), which reported a 300,000\(\times\) increase in compute used in the largest AI training runs over that six-year window (Amodei and Hernandez 2018); comparisons that extend to later frontier models span a longer window and report larger factors. The Green AI proposal (reporting FLOPs alongside accuracy for every published result) reframes efficiency from an engineering preference into a scientific reporting obligation, making the resource cost of marginal accuracy gains visible and comparable across research groups.

Schwartz, Roy, Jesse Dodge, Noah A. Smith, and Oren Etzioni. 2020. “Green AI.” Communications of the ACM 63 (12): 54–63. https://doi.org/10.1145/3381831.
Amodei, Dario, and Danny Hernandez. 2018. “AI and Compute.” OpenAI Blog 6.

↩︎ The bitter lesson, the historical pattern in which methods that scale with compute overtake hand-built knowledge, is established in The Bitter Lesson.

The growth in training compute carries a price, and the price decides who can take part. Figure 6 plots the estimated compute cost of training notable models since 2016 in constant 2023 dollars. Across the models that Epoch AI flags as frontier at release, the cost of a training run rose about 2.9× per year, and the most expensive run in the data, Grok 4 in 2025, cost about $388M (Epoch AI 2026). Cottier et al. (2024), using their own selection of frontier runs, estimate 2.4\(\times\) per year. Either rate concentrates frontier training in the few organizations able to fund it, so efficiency is a question of access as well as of energy. For a frontier model the training run dominates the cost; for a high-traffic service such as DLRM, as section 15.4.2 shows, serving does.

At the hardware level, environmental impact scales with total electrical energy draw across the machine lifecycle, mediated by accelerator utilization, data-center cooling overhead (power usage effectiveness), and the carbon intensity of the local electrical grid, the mass of CO2eq emitted per kilowatt-hour it delivers. Within the accelerator package, data movement can dominate energy dissipation. In the 45 nm reference behind the energy-movement principle (7), a single off-chip DRAM access costs two to three orders of magnitude more energy than a 32-bit floating-point operation, although total energy also depends on access width, event counts, reuse, and the device. Algorithmic and runtime optimizations reduce environmental burden only when they reduce measured watt-hours for a verified quality target under production load, curbing this physical dissipation at the silicon level.

↩︎ The physical energy disparity between off-chip memory access and arithmetic execution is established in Machine learning hardware specialization.

Physical resource demands also dictate deployment accessibility. Oversized models that need multi-accelerator nodes linked by high-bandwidth interconnects (such as NVLink) only to hold their weights in memory confine deployment to centralized cloud facilities. Compressing models so their working sets fit within commodity DRAM or the unified memory of edge processors opens up who can run them. Running locally removes the need for a constant high-bandwidth network link and recurring cloud API bills, letting client devices run low-latency, private inference right where data is captured.

At production scale, where serving systems process millions of requests daily, per-query inefficiencies compound rapidly across accelerator fleets. Under the iron law of ML systems (\(\Time = \DataVolume / \Bandwidth + \Operations / (\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw) + \Latency\)), per-query execution latency dictates the request throughput that an individual accelerator can sustain before violating service-level latency objectives. Reducing execution time allows a smaller provisioned cluster to support the same aggregate traffic, and capacity planning can turn that reduced service demand into lower provisioned-capacity costs. A fixed fleet may also save dynamic energy, although idle and baseline power remain. A valid comparison therefore measures total energy over the same traffic and quality target and distinguishes that result from savings on a capacity-based bill.

Each optimization developed earlier in this book attacks a specific term of these equations, and each reduces environmental burden only under the conditions its own chapter states. Quantization and pruning shrink the bytes moved \(\DataVolume\) and the operation count \(\Operations\) when supported kernels retain the smaller representation, and knowledge distillation moves a large model’s behavior into a smaller one (Model Compression). Selecting the data a model trains on removes training operations before they run (Data Selection), and specialized datapaths and fused kernels raise hardware efficiency \(\EfficiencyHw\) (Hardware Acceleration).

Responsible engineering treats these optimizations as design constraints established before training and deployment, rather than post-hoc remedies applied to an oversized model. System requirements specify explicit upper bounds on latency, memory capacity, and energy consumption alongside target predictive quality, and the task is to find the most compact architecture and execution strategy that satisfies them. In practice this begins by establishing bounded machine envelopes (thermal design power, battery capacity, and peak latency) before choosing or training an architecture. Targeted optimizations such as quantization or kernel fusion come only after the most compact adequate architecture has been selected.

Edge deployment makes these limits immediate, because passive thermal dissipation and battery capacity enforce power budgets that cannot be relaxed after the fact. A wearable with an illustrative 500 mW inference budget shows the consequence for model choice. Under the active-power profiles assumed here, the KWS lighthouse’s DS-CNN (200K parameters at 50 mW) leaves a 10× power margin, while MobileNetV2 (3.5M parameters at 1.2 W) exceeds the same budget by 2.4× before sustained thermals are considered. Fitting the envelope is necessary but not sufficient: a keyword detector does not substitute for a vision model merely because it fits, and per-inference energy compounds into aggregate emissions and operating cost once the model is deployed at scale. Techniques that lower measured energy per inference reduce lifecycle energy only when their savings outweigh any added training, preprocessing, or other workload costs, while cloud and cluster financial savings depend directly on provisioning and hardware utilization.

↩︎ The power, memory, and latency envelopes that separate cloud, edge, mobile, and TinyML deployment are established in Physical Constraints on Deployment.

15.4.2 Total cost of ownership

On the DLRM ranking service, a training cycle that costs $3,200 is followed by roughly $500,000 per year in serving compute. That contrast shows how total cost of ownership,14 the upfront and recurring expense of a system over its lifetime, can be dominated by recurring inference at high traffic. Where the cloud-versus-edge comparison used that total to choose a deployment, the lifecycle view splits one deployment’s total into training cycles, recurring inference, and operations to find which term dominates. Other systems may be dominated by training, data, staffing, or idle capacity, so measurement determines where optimization should focus.

14 TCO (total cost of ownership): ML TCO also includes labeling, monitoring, retraining, remediation, energy, audits, and compliance, costs that an estimate of compute alone omits. Utilization and update cadence determine how the balance between training and inference falls for a given service.

At production scale, recurring inference can dominate lifetime cost.

↩︎ Total cost of ownership as a comparison of cloud and edge deployments, weighing capital against operating expense, is established in Cloud ML trade-offs and constraints.

Walking the DLRM service’s numbers for its 10 million daily users shows why those two terms sit so far apart. A single training cycle appears considerable on its own: data preparation consumes 100 GPU-hours at approximately $4/hour ($400), hyperparameter search across multiple configurations requires 500 GPU-hours ($2,000), and the final training run uses 200 GPU-hours ($800), for a cycle total of $3,200.

Recurring inference dwarfs that figure because the 10 million daily users and 20 ranked items per user from the exposure-feedback analysis require 200 million inferences every day. Treating 10 milliseconds per inference as unbatched, non-overlapped dedicated GPU service demand yields approximately 23.1 GPUs running continuously. At $2.50/GPU-hour, the scenario’s annual GPU cost reaches $506,944. That rate sits below the $4/hour the training runs pay because an always-on fleet is bought as continuously reserved capacity rather than on demand.

Over a three-year operational period, twelve total quarterly training cycles, including the initial cycle, produce training costs of approximately $38,400, while inference costs over the same period total $1.5M. The resulting 40:1 ratio is specific to these assumptions, but it dictates where optimization effort yields the highest return: inference latency and serving efficiency.

↩︎ Cost per inference, the serving cost that capacity planning divides among requests, is established in Cost per inference across hardware.

Per-query optimization becomes important when serving billions of requests. Shaving a fifth off the per-query accelerator service demand can reduce required hardware when other workload assumptions remain fixed. Hardware selection among CPUs, GPUs, and Tensor Processing Units changes costs and carbon footprint in workload- and deployment-dependent ways. Model compression through quantization and pruning can reduce high-volume inference costs when it lowers measured service demand without unacceptable quality loss.

The third term of the lifecycle total cost of ownership (TCO) is operations: monitoring, maintenance, retraining, and incident response, all of which scale with system complexity and with the rate of distribution shift in the application domain. Resources consumed by one ML system also cannot be used for other purposes, so wasteful consumption in one project constrains what other projects can attempt. Engineers should therefore evaluate return on investment (ROI), asking whether the value an ML system delivers justifies its resource consumption. A recommendation system that increases engagement by 1 percent might not justify millions of dollars in computational costs, while a medical diagnosis system that saves lives does. Deployment-to-training cost ratios vary with traffic, utilization, hardware, update cadence, staffing, and incident burden, so the right model choice depends on measured lifecycle cost and task value, not on a fixed ratio.

Accounting for cost and carbon

Converting the same scenario into carbon requires measured or estimated energy use, facility overhead, and applicable grid carbon intensity. The accounting keeps the walk-through’s traffic, service demand, and training budget, adds the operations work that the walk-through left unquantified, and converts every accelerator-hour into emissions with one conversion factor. Training recurs with each retraining cycle, inference recurs with every request, and operations accumulate through telemetry, on-call engineering, and incident response, so each enters the three-year total on its own basis.

Napkin Math 15.4: The carbon cost of compute
Problem: The cost accounting must convert GPU-only electrical energy into “kg CO2eq.” What carbon factor does one GPU-hour carry under the scenario baseline, excluding host, facility, and embodied impacts?

Variables:

  • Power: 400 W per GPU (scenario baseline).
  • Intensity: 0.4 kg/kWh CO2eq (rounded grid baseline).

Math: Equation 2 captures the standard conversion: \[ \text{Carbon} = \text{Energy (kWh)} \times \text{Carbon Intensity (kg/kWh)} \tag{2}\] Applying the baseline assumptions: \[\begin{gather*} \left(\text{0.4 kW} \times \text{1 hour}\right) \times \text{0.4 kg/kWh} = \text{0.16 kg CO}_2\text{eq per GPU-hour} \end{gather*}\] Systems insight: This GPU-only conversion factor lets the accounting track a modeled accelerator-carbon subtotal alongside dollar cost. Whole-system and facility emissions require incorporating the host, facility overhead, and embodied energy boundaries.

In table 10, inference accounts for 73.5 percent of the three-year cost, operations for 24.6 percent, and training for only 1.9 percent. Those proportions turn efficiency from a tuning preference into a responsibility check, because small serving-path reductions compound across every request the fleet handles.

Checkpoint 15.4: Efficiency as responsibility

Total cost of ownership reveals where responsible optimization has the most leverage.

15.4.3 Environmental impact

Two data centers can run the exact same DLRM serving fleet at the same dollar bill while one emits several times more carbon than the other because its local power grid burns coal instead of hydroelectricity. A team that replaces working servers early to save 10 percent on electricity can raise net lifetime emissions once the carbon spent manufacturing the new silicon is counted (Gupta et al. 2022). The TCO accounting in section 15.4.2 paired billed compute dollars with a GPU-only carbon subtotal under one baseline grid, but full environmental accounting must include physical overheads that no cloud invoice itemizes. Operational emissions depend on whole-system energy, facility power usage effectiveness (PUE), and regional grid carbon intensity (\(\text{gCO}_2\text{e}/\text{kWh}\)), while embodied manufacturing impacts require separate accounting. In power benchmarking, PUE separates server-plug energy from facility overhead. In carbon accounting, the same ratio, total facility energy divided by IT-equipment energy, becomes the multiplier that converts measured IT energy into the energy the facility actually draws. The same measurement boundary keeps server fans inside the IT term so that their energy is not counted twice.

Gupta, Udit, Young Geun Kim, Sylvia Lee, Jordan Tse, Hsien-Hsin S Lee, Gu-Yeon Wei, David Brooks, and Carole-Jean Wu. 2022. “Chasing Carbon: The Elusive Environmental Footprint of Computing.” IEEE Micro 42 (4): 37–47. https://doi.org/10.1109/MM.2022.3163226.

↩︎ The power measurement boundary that separates server-plug energy from facility overhead is established in Power measurement boundaries.

Henderson, Peter, Jieru Hu, Joshua Romoff, Emma Brunskill, Dan Jurafsky, and Joelle Pineau. 2020. “Towards the Systematic Reporting of the Energy and Carbon Footprints of Machine Learning.” CoRR abs/2002.05651 (248): 1–43. https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.2002.05651.

A first-order operational emissions estimate is \(\text{Energy}_{\text{hardware}} \times \text{PUE} \times \text{Carbon Intensity}\), where the hardware term covers the full IT-equipment boundary over the accounting window, not GPU-only energy. Operational carbon includes electricity consumed by both active work and idle provisioned equipment. In contrast, embodied carbon encompasses the greenhouse gases emitted across the hardware supply chain (silicon wafer fabrication, packaging, transport, and facility construction) before a chip executes its first cycle. Embodied carbon can account for a large share of lifetime emissions, and on low-carbon grids a majority (Gupta et al. 2022), so prematurely replacing older servers for slight operational efficiency gains can paradoxically increase net lifetime emissions. Per-request energy is measured in joules per inference, whereas joules per FLOP characterizes hardware work efficiency. An optimization that reduces TCO may not reduce energy or emissions if it changes utilization, hardware, or workload placement. Data-center electricity use makes workload design, hardware, cloud region, and timing part of responsible engineering (Henderson et al. 2020).

The DLRM accounting in table 10 shows how lopsided the carbon split can be. Under its service-demand assumptions, inference emits 63× as much modeled accelerator CO2 as all 12 training cycles combined, so for a service of this kind the serving path, not the training run, is where carbon reductions have leverage. Efficiency optimization and environmental responsibility align when measured lifecycle energy and emissions fall for the required workload and quality target. Broader accounting frameworks extend that measurement across time and organizational boundaries: lifecycle assessment sums impact from hardware manufacture through decommissioning, Scope 1, 2, and 3 reporting separates direct site emissions from purchased electricity and supply-chain or use-phase footprints, and carbon-aware scheduling acts on those signals by shifting batch jobs to lower-carbon hours or regions.

The same physical quantities that govern performance also affect responsibility. Data movement and computation contribute to chip-level energy, while data-center emissions additionally depend on utilization, facility overhead, grid intensity, and embodied impacts. Pareto analysis applies to fairness, latency, and carbon alike, and the first question is always whether the current design lies on the frontier, because a dominated design can improve several objectives at once. Responsible engineering extends the constrained optimization problem this book has been teaching to objectives that include societal impact alongside throughput and latency. The magnitude of the carbon term becomes clearer in a scale calculation for training a large foundation model; under the grid assumption of napkin math 15.5, one such run exceeds a hundred passenger-car years of emissions (see the margin).

This training scenario exceeds 100 passenger-car years of emissions.

Napkin Math 15.5: The carbon cost of scale
Problem: A foundation model is being trained at the scale of GPT-3, consuming 1,287 MWh of electricity (Patterson et al. 2021). What is the operational carbon impact under the stated grid-intensity assumption?

Math:

  1. Energy consumption: 1,287 MWh = 1,287,000 kWh.
  2. Carbon intensity: This scenario uses \(\approx\) 429 g/kWh CO2 (0.429 kg/kWh) (Patterson et al. 2021), a US grid-average figure slightly above the rounded 0.4 kg/kWh baseline of the cost accounting. Each calculation states its own grid assumption, and the difference changes the result by less than a tenth.
  3. Total emissions: 1,287,000 kWh \(\times\) 0.429 kg/kWh = 552,123 kg CO2 (552 t).
  4. Comparison: The scenario assumes 4.6 t CO2 per passenger-car year. This per-year basis differs from the lifetime-car basis of the 2019 five-car estimate, so the two car comparisons are not directly comparable.

Systems insight: Under this training-energy scenario, the operational emissions equal the assumed annual emissions of 120 passenger cars. A 1 percent reduction in total training energy under the same grid mix would remove the equivalent of about 1.2 cars taken off the road for a year from the calculation.

Patterson, David, Joseph Gonzalez, Quoc Le, Chen Liang, Lluis-Miquel Munguia, Daniel Rothchild, David So, Maud Texier, and Jeff Dean. 2021. “Carbon Emissions and Large Neural Network Training.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2104.10350.

Pricing compute in kilowatt-hours, tonnes of CO2, and three-year operating dollars places resource consumption alongside fairness and explainability as a measurable boundary of the system. Specification, measurement, documentation, and resource accounting together define what a responsible system must satisfy, but none of those bounds is self-verifying. The Govern stage of the lifecycle makes them provable to someone outside the team through infrastructure that controls data access, bounds privacy leakage, and preserves lineage and audit evidence in production.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team optimizes an inference model using INT8 quantization and structured pruning, reducing dedicated accelerator compute by \(4\times\) while preserving accuracy. According to the chapter, why is this efficiency improvement classified as a responsible engineering intervention rather than a pure performance optimization?

    1. Because quantization mathematically guarantees that disparate impact across all protected demographic groups drops to zero.
    2. Because reducing model parameters eliminates the need for data governance and audit logging in production pipelines.
    3. Because efficiency optimizations are relevant only for one-time training runs, where carbon emissions are legally regulated.
    4. Because it can lower lifecycle energy and cost and broaden hardware access when the actual runtime reduces measured service demand at the required quality and the deployment converts that gain into savings.

  2. A wearable device has an inference power budget of 500 mW. Under the chapter’s assumed active-power profiles (KWS DS-CNN: 50 mW; MobileNetV2: 1.2 W), which statement about the resource screen is correct?

    1. MobileNetV2 passes, because its power overage can always be removed by offloading inference to the cloud.
    2. The DS-CNN passes with a 10\(\times\) power margin and MobileNetV2 exceeds the budget by 2.4\(\times\); passing the screen still does not establish that the DS-CNN suits the intended task.
    3. Both pass, because duty cycling makes any active power draw acceptable on a wearable.
    4. Neither passes, because no ML model can run within a sub-watt budget.

  3. In the chapter’s illustrative three-year TCO model of the DLRM ranking service (Training: ~2%, Operations: ~25%, Inference: ~73%), assume one intervention halves total training cost, while another reduces total inference cost by 20% under unchanged traffic and quality. Which intervention saves more dollars, and by what approximate ratio? Why would halving only the final training run time not establish the first saving?

  4. True or False: For an identical serving workload, relocating an inference deployment from a carbon-intensive fossil-fuel grid region to a region powered predominantly by low-carbon renewable energy can reduce operational carbon emissions more than a modest algorithmic efficiency improvement.

  5. In data-center environmental accounting, the metric defined as the ratio of total facility energy to the energy consumed specifically by computing equipment is known as ____ (abbreviated PUE).

15.5 Data Governance and Compliance

The keyword spotting system makes the governance problem physical. An always-listening voice assistant samples audio continuously in private homes, stores enrolled speaker profiles, and periodically syncs with cloud pipelines that retrain on population-wide audio. Every guarantee the earlier stages established now therefore depends on what data the system may hold, who may touch it, and what evidence survives. Data governance, the engineering discipline that turns regulatory and ethical obligations into verifiable technical controls, is the enforcement layer that makes accountability operational. Fairness metrics, model cards, and impact assessments have practical force only if the system can prove what data it ingested, which principals accessed it, which legal basis authorized processing, and whether applicable deletion or contestability requests were executed. The legal-basis question alone can decide an enforcement outcome. In 2023, Ireland’s Data Protection Commission found that Meta had relied on an invalid legal basis for personalized advertising and had violated transparency and fairness requirements, a governance failure that involved no security breach at all (Data Protection Commission 2023).

Data Protection Commission. 2023. Data Protection Commission Announces Conclusion of Two Inquiries into Meta Ireland. Regulatory press release.

Storage architectures also serve as governance enforcement mechanisms that determine which principals access data, how queries are tracked, and whether pipelines satisfy legal requirements. Every architectural decision, from ingestion strategies through extract, transform, load (ETL) pipelines to persistent storage layout, carries governance implications that surface during regulatory audits, privacy investigations, or model recalls. Data governance translates abstract policy into concrete systems engineering through authenticated access controls on training partitions, immutable audit infrastructure that logs dataset access, privacy-preserving transformations during feature extraction, and deterministic lineage systems that trace training snapshots to deployed model checkpoints.

↩︎ The database, warehouse, and data-lake storage architectures, and the access patterns each one serves, are established in Storage system options.

Principle: Compliance as engineering constraint
Invariant: Data governance obligations are engineering requirements, not optional policy overlays.

Implication: Systems that process regulated data must map applicable duties to technical and organizational controls. Access control, erasure, contestability, audit, and lineage may be required or useful depending on the jurisdiction, data, actor, and use case. The General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), California Consumer Privacy Act (CCPA), Brazil’s General Data Protection Law (LGPD, Lei Geral de Proteção de Dados), and China’s Personal Information Protection Law (PIPL) differ in scope and duties, so no single control checklist establishes compliance across them.

Data governance turns security, privacy, compliance, and lineage into mutually dependent engineering constraints, and the KWS system shows where each one binds. Continuous capture, enrolled profiles, and fleet-wide retraining create obligations across consent management, data minimization, access auditing, and deletion rights, and the four domains map directly to hardware and pipeline enforcement points in this architecture. Security comes first by protecting raw features, training partitions, and model artifacts across the ingestion lifecycle through mutual authentication, role-based authorization, encryption at rest and in transit, and hardware-rooted key management. Hardware keystores on edge microcontrollers protect stored speaker templates, mutual TLS authenticates telemetry in transit, and role-based access controls gate access to centralized training audio in cloud storage. Because access control alone does not limit what an authenticated consumer can learn from the records it reads, privacy regulates information exposure inside that boundary through differential privacy, k-anonymity, and automated feature redaction. These measures keep ambient KWS audio inside a circular SRAM buffer until an on-device trigger and bound information leakage during fleet-wide acoustic model retraining.

Where privacy governs what leaves the device, compliance translates jurisdiction-specific statutes (such as GDPR, CCPA, and LGPD) into retention windows, consent verification, and cross-border replication rules once data arrives in the cloud. Compliance thereby determines the legal basis for processing and the scope of any revocation or erasure workflow that removes in-scope voice profiles and acoustic data across primary stores and backups. Proving that those security, privacy, and retention rules were followed requires lineage and audit systems that render the entire data lifecycle inspectable. Retained lineage linking deployed weights through training snapshots to authorized source records supports verification of security claims and applicable obligations. In the KWS pipeline, such lineage establishes provenance through content hashes and signed manifests that link each deployed DS-CNN binary back through specific training splits and acoustic augmentation seeds to the original raw recordings.

Governance works as an operating model rather than a single control. The framework in figure 7 arranges eight elements, from organizational oversight and compliance policies to data catalogs, sourcing, quality metrics, runtime operations, and security, around a central governance hub. Technical mechanisms and organizational processes must function as mutually reinforcing subsystems: encrypted storage without identity-mapped access controls still lets an insider copy data out, and compliant storage without verifiable audit trails cannot pass a regulatory inspection. Within the D·A·M taxonomy, data governance is the control plane of the Data axis. It enforces lawful sourcing, privacy bounds, and lifecycle integrity for the data that feeds the Algorithm, and it runs on Machine-axis infrastructure such as encrypted storage, access control, and audit logs.

Engineering teams do not operate in isolation. Team-level technical practices such as audit logging, bias testing, and explainable interfaces sit at the core of expanding organizational, industry, and regulatory structures (figure 8). Requirements and legal mandates flow inward across each boundary, and the technical controls at the center supply the evidence that external compliance rests on. The rest of this section works at that innermost ring, starting with who may reach the data at all.

15.5.1 Security and access control architecture

When a data scientist queries the KWS feature store for training data, she can read aggregated voice features but cannot access the raw audio recordings from which they were derived. The serving pipeline, by contrast, can read online features for inference without writing to the training dataset or modifying source data. That separation reflects a layered security architecture in which governance requirements translate into enforceable technical controls at each pipeline stage. Feature stores enforce role-based access control (RBAC) by mapping organizational policies to authenticated identities and database permissions. These controls operate across distinct storage tiers. Object storage enforces bucket-level access policies, data warehouses implement column-level security and dynamic masking for sensitive fields, and feature stores maintain isolated read and write paths that separate low-latency online inference key-value lookups from high-throughput offline batch training extracts.

↩︎ The dual-storage architecture of offline columnar stores and online key-value caches is established in Feature stores.

Access control mechanisms remain incomplete without encryption, which protects data at rest and in transit but does not prevent misuse after decryption or authorized access. Training corpora in data lakes use envelope encryption with keys held in a dedicated key management service. Feature stores encrypt tables at rest and authenticate every service-to-service transfer. KWS edge devices combine transport encryption for payload confidentiality with asymmetric code signing to verify model-update integrity. Each of these controls is only as strong as its key management and update authorization.

Protecting data while it is being computed on is a further layer, confidential computing. Hardware secure enclaves and trusted execution environments isolate protected memory and execution from host software outside their trust boundary, and attestation lets a client verify the approved configuration before releasing keys or data to it. The guarantee covers only the declared attacker boundary and supported configuration, and it does not protect data from the application authorized to process it. The technique matters most when training and serving run on infrastructure that the data owner does not control.

Serialized weights and checkpoints require the same protection as training data. Deserializing an unverified checkpoint in a format that can embed executable objects (Python pickle archives are the common case) gives an attacker arbitrary code execution on training nodes and inference workers. Restricted serialization formats that store only tensors avoid that mechanism, and write-protected promotion gates with cryptographic signatures verify artifact integrity and authorized provenance before loading. These controls do not establish that a model is free of backdoors or that every parser and runtime is secure.

Authorized loading also leaves the model’s behavior to be evaluated. Poisoned training samples can induce backdoors, perturbed inference inputs can induce misclassification (evasion attacks), and systematic queries can extract model functionality (Tramèr et al. 2016). Access control, cryptographic enclaves, and artifact verification therefore establish who can reach data and models and how they are protected across storage, transit, and execution, yet controlling access is only half the problem. Even authorized callers can compromise individual privacy if the data or model outputs themselves expose too much. Prediction APIs or raw weights can reveal whether a particular record was present in training (Shokri et al. 2017), so artifact integrity and limits on information exposure require distinct controls.

Tramèr, Florian, Fan Zhang, Ari Juels, Michael K Reiter, and Thomas Ristenpart. 2016. “Stealing Machine Learning Models via Prediction APIs.” 25th USENIX Security Symposium (USENIX Security 16), 601–18.

15.5.2 Technical privacy protection methods

Even when role-based access control works as designed, a data scientist querying a training table does not need, and should not see, individual user records when aggregate statistics suffice, nor should a released checkpoint or prediction API reveal whether a specific customer’s record was in the training set (Shokri et al. 2017). Privacy-preserving techniques15 close that exposure gap by bounding what queries and model outputs reveal even to authorized callers. Differential privacy bounds changes in an exported output distribution under a specified neighboring-dataset relation.16 One training mechanism is Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (DP-SGD) (Abadi et al. 2016). Unlike standard gradient clipping, which caps the batch-averaged gradient for numerical stability, DP-SGD clips each individual sample’s gradient to an \(\ell_2\) norm threshold \(\GradientClippingNorm\) before summing across the batch. Bounding each sample’s contribution is necessary because an unclipped outlier record could shift the batch sum by an arbitrarily large amount, leaving no finite noise scale able to hide whether that record was in the batch. Once every sample’s influence is bounded by \(\GradientClippingNorm\), DP-SGD adds calibrated Gaussian noise with distribution \(\GaussianDistribution(0, \PrivacyNoiseMultiplier^2 \GradientClippingNorm^2 \IdentityMatrix)\) to the sum of clipped gradients, where \(\PrivacyNoiseMultiplier\) is the noise multiplier. A privacy accountant tracks the privacy loss composed across the sampled training steps and checks it against the chosen epsilon budget.

15 Privacy-preserving techniques: K-anonymity (Sweeney 2002) ensures each record is indistinguishable from at least \(\AnonymitySetSize-1\) others with respect to chosen quasi-identifiers, l-diversity adds attribute variety within equivalence classes (Machanavajjhala et al. 2007), and t-closeness bounds distribution distance (Li et al. 2007). These syntactic guarantees address different threats from differential privacy and do not by themselves protect against all ML-specific leakage: a model trained on de-identified data can still memorize examples or reveal membership signal under some conditions (Shokri et al. 2017). Differential privacy instead bounds the influence of one record under a specified mechanism and privacy budget, offering a semantic guarantee designed to remain meaningful in the presence of side information; the methods are therefore not a simple strength hierarchy, and the appropriate protection depends on the release, threat model, and utility requirements.

Sweeney, Latanya. 2002. “K-ANONYMITY: A MODEL for PROTECTING PRIVACY.” International Journal of Uncertainty, Fuzziness and Knowledge-Based Systems 10 (05): 557–70. https://doi.org/10.1142/s0218488502001648.
Machanavajjhala, Ashwin, Daniel Kifer, Johannes Gehrke, and Muthu Venkitasubramaniam. 2007. “l-Diversity: Privacy Beyond k-Anonymity.” ACM Transactions on Knowledge Discovery from Data 1 (3): 3. https://doi.org/10.1145/1217299.1217302.
Li, Ninghui, Tiancheng Li, and Suresh Venkatasubramanian. 2007. “t-Closeness: Privacy Beyond k-Anonymity and l-Diversity.” 2007 IEEE 23rd International Conference on Data Engineering, 106–15. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICDE.2007.367856.

16 Differential privacy: Introduced by Dwork et al. (2006), a randomized mechanism \(\RandomizedMechanism\) satisfies \((\PrivacyBudget, \PrivacyAdditiveSlack)\)-differential privacy if for all neighboring datasets \(\NeighboringDataset, \AdjacentNeighboringDataset\) differing by one record and output sets \(\MechanismOutputSet\), \(\Pr(\RandomizedMechanism(\NeighboringDataset) \in \MechanismOutputSet) \leq e^\PrivacyBudget\cdot \Pr(\RandomizedMechanism(\AdjacentNeighboringDataset) \in \MechanismOutputSet) + \PrivacyAdditiveSlack\). Here \(\PrivacyBudget\) controls the multiplicative bound and \(\PrivacyAdditiveSlack\) is additive slack in the inequality; their acceptable values depend on the neighboring-dataset relation, release, and policy. The systems trade-off is utility rather than mere implementation complexity: stronger privacy often requires more noise or tighter sampling, and privacy loss composes across repeated queries or training steps. Engineers must therefore track cumulative privacy loss with a valid accountant and report the assumptions behind the chosen budget.

Dwork, Cynthia, Frank McSherry, Kobbi Nissim, and Adam Smith. 2006. “Calibrating Noise to Sensitivity in Private Data Analysis.” In Theory of Cryptography Conference (TCC), edited by Shai Halevi and Tal Rabin, vol. 3876. Lecture Notes in Computer Science. Springer Berlin Heidelberg. https://doi.org/10.1007/11681878_14.
Abadi, M., A. Chu, I. Goodfellow, H. B. McMahan, I. Mironov, K. Talwar, and L. Zhang. 2016. “Deep Learning with Differential Privacy.” Proceedings of the 2016 ACM SIGSAC Conference on Computer and Communications Security, CCS ’16, 308–18. https://doi.org/10.1145/2976749.2978318.

Standard backpropagation kernels sum weight gradients across the batch dimension in place as they execute, so they never expose an individual sample’s gradient norm. Clipping needs each sample’s gradient norm before that sum occurs, and the direct way to obtain it materializes one full gradient buffer per sample, so gradient storage grows by the batch size. For the KWS acoustic model (200K parameters), one FP32 gradient occupies 800 KB and a batch of 256 per-sample gradients 204.8 MB. The same approach for the 1.5B-parameter GPT-2 would need 1.5 TB, far beyond the 80 GB of one H100. Specialized two-pass clipping implementations compute each sample’s gradient norm directly from layer activations and backpropagated output gradients before accumulating the rescaled weight updates, avoiding per-sample weight-gradient buffers, and small sequential microbatches trade the memory back for lost parallelism. The cost of DP-SGD must therefore be measured for the chosen implementation with its clipping, noise, sampling, and accounting assumptions intact.

Empirical attacks complement these formal checks. Membership inference attacks measure whether an observer can tell that a specific record was in the training set, and model inversion attacks use confidence outputs to reconstruct sensitive feature values or a training subject’s likeness (Fredrikson et al. 2015). A successful attack reveals practical leakage, but no empirical attack can prove that an \((\PrivacyBudget, \PrivacyAdditiveSlack)\) bound holds.17 Reducing confidence-score precision or restricting outputs to top-\(\TopClassCount\) classes reduces what some APIs expose, but neither is a complete defense. Their value depends on the attacker’s access and auxiliary information, while differential privacy provides a separate bound under its stated mechanism and accounting assumptions.

Fredrikson, Matt, Somesh Jha, and Thomas Ristenpart. 2015. “Model Inversion Attacks That Exploit Confidence Information and Basic Countermeasures.” Proceedings of the 22nd ACM SIGSAC Conference on Computer and Communications Security, 1322–33. https://doi.org/10.1145/2810103.2813677.

17 Membership inference attack: The attack uses model behavior to infer whether a record appeared in training; overfitting can increase the signal (Shokri et al. 2017). In practice, higher confidence on training samples can help distinguish members from nonmembers. Its measured success depends on model access, data distribution, and defense assumptions.

Shokri, Reza, Marco Stronati, Congzheng Song, and Vitaly Shmatikov. 2017. “Membership Inference Attacks Against Machine Learning Models.” 2017 IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy (SP), 3–18. https://doi.org/10.1109/SP.2017.41.

While differential privacy bounds information leakage from model outputs, collecting continuous raw sensor streams creates an immediate vulnerability if raw data leaves the capture device. Always-listening edge architectures, such as the lighthouse KWS system, operate under severe privacy constraints because continuous ambient microphone sampling must coexist with strict minimization and retention boundaries. To reduce routine transmission exposure, this design confines acoustic feature extraction and wake-word detection to local low-power microcontrollers or digital signal processors (DSPs). Incoming audio samples stream through a small volatile circular buffer in on-chip SRAM, where older frames are continuously overwritten by newly sampled pulse-code modulation (PCM) data. The policy allows saving or sending audio only after an on-device wake-word trigger. Because detection can produce false wakes, validation must cover both the detector and the buffer and egress controls, including which pre-trigger audio becomes accessible after activation.

Confining raw audio to the device enforces data minimization but starves centralized training of the acoustic variety that improves accuracy. Federated learning recovers that variety by moving training to the data. Training-capable clients such as paired phones or home hubs train on authorized local examples and send model updates instead of raw audio, and a coordinator combines those updates into a shared model.18 The constrained KWS microcontroller only runs the result. The approach exchanges centralization risk for physical bottlenecks. Every round moves parameter updates over constrained wireless uplinks, battery-powered clients drop out mid-round, data that differ from home to home make local updates diverge, and raw updates can still leak training examples through reconstruction attacks (Zhu et al. 2019). Compression of those updates reduces communication but provides no privacy guarantee by itself.

18 Federated learning: McMahan et al. (2017) introduced Federated Averaging (FedAvg), in which each client runs several local training steps before the coordinator averages the resulting models. Averaging after many local steps rather than after every gradient cuts the number of communication rounds, which is what makes training over constrained client uplinks practical.

McMahan, Brendan, Eider Moore, Daniel Ramage, Seth Hampson, and Blaise Agüera y Arcas. 2017. “Communication-Efficient Learning of Deep Networks from Decentralized Data.” International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 54: 1273–82.
Zhu, Ligeng, Zhijian Liu, and Song Han. 2019. “Deep Leakage from Gradients.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 32: 14774–84.
Bonawitz, Keith, Vladimir Ivanov, Ben Kreuter, Antonio Marcedone, H. Brendan McMahan, Sarvar Patel, Daniel Ramage, Aaron Segal, and Karn Seth. 2017. “Practical Secure Aggregation for Privacy-Preserving Machine Learning.” Proceedings of the 2017 ACM SIGSAC Conference on Computer and Communications Security, 1175–91. https://doi.org/10.1145/3133956.3133982.

Secure aggregation lets the coordinator learn only the aggregate of participating clients’ updates, under the protocol’s participation, dropout, and collusion assumptions (Bonawitz et al. 2017). What that aggregate and the final model reveal remains outside its protection; differential privacy bounds that release by limiting the effect of any one record or client, provided the training mechanism and privacy accountant support the chosen neighboring relation. A design that needs both must account separately for what a curious coordinator observes during training and what recipients can infer from released checkpoints.

When triggered audio recordings or aggregated model checkpoints must be retained on centralized infrastructure for verification or downstream training, privacy protection shifts to automated lifecycle management. Automated retention workflows apply time-to-live policies across primary stores, caches, training partitions, and backups. Cryptographic erasure destroys the keys needed to decrypt covered copies; physical overwrite is a distinct storage-sanitization mechanism. Neither removes every copy automatically. The workflow must inventory replicas, key copies, and backups, define applicable retention exceptions, and verify that in-scope data is no longer recoverable under the chosen threat model. Differential privacy, on-device minimization, federated training, and automated retention supply the technical mechanisms that bound exposure, while external statutes decide which of those mechanisms are mandatory and what rights affected people can invoke.

15.5.3 Regulation as architecture

When the credit service denies an applicant who then asks why, contests the decision, and requests access to or deletion of the data used about her, those retention, erasure, and explanation paths stop being internal engineering preferences and become statutory rights. To satisfy them, the system must do more than report an accuracy score. It must produce an explanation tied to the specific decision, preserve the model and data lineage that led to that output, route the dispute to a substantive human review path, retain audit logs, and support deletion or access workflows where data rights apply. Explicit regulatory frameworks turn transparency, oversight, and accountability into non-negotiable architecture requirements.

Regulation first enters the architecture through risk classification. The EU AI Act establishes a comprehensive framework, classifying AI systems by risk level and mandating requirements accordingly.19 The Act entered into force on August 1, 2024 and applies in phases: prohibited-practice rules began applying in 2025, while high-risk and other operator obligations phase in by system category and implementation guidance (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2024).

19 EU AI Act (Regulation 2024/1689): The first comprehensive AI legal framework, defining four risk tiers with penalties that vary by infringement category. The Act has extraterritorial reach: non-EU organizations may need to comply when they place systems on the EU market or when system outputs are used in the EU. Systems engineering implications are concrete: high-risk AI requires logging infrastructure for audit trails, human oversight mechanisms built into the architecture, and CE marking (the EU conformity mark), all capabilities that must be designed in from inception, not retrofitted after deployment.

European Parliament and Council of the European Union. 2024. Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 13 June 2024 Laying down Harmonised Rules on Artificial Intelligence (AI Act). Official Journal of the European Union, L 2024/1689.

20 High-risk AI (EU AI Act Annex III): Annex III enumerates specified use cases in areas including biometrics, critical infrastructure, education, employment, essential services, law enforcement, migration, and justice. Classification depends on intended use and the Article 6 criteria, including exclusions for some systems that do not materially influence decisions or pose significant risk; profiling systems within Annex III remain high-risk. Model architecture alone does not determine classification.
